Signature Ecosystem Maturity: Universal Ecosystem Generator

JAH-UEGS-000023 · Ecosystem Maturity

Problem: The problem this design addresses is the absence of a worked design for Ecosystem Maturity inside the Universal Ecosystem Generator. A design-study answers that absence with a proposal that can be read, criticized, and improved, without pretending the proposal is a finished system. The purpose of this database is to record how a whole ecosystem is generated: not one database or one website, but a connected network of databases, artificial intelligences, and applications that share records, interfaces, and a dependency registry. A single generator can make a single thing; an ecosystem generator makes the network that holds many things together, so that a new database arrives already linked to its website, its generator, its artificial intelligence, and its tests. Without that, every new resource must be wired in by hand, and the wiring is where most failures happen. Every record here specifies one part of ecosystem generation completely enough that a builder could implement it and a checker could test it. As a design, it is judged on whether it is complete, coherent, and testable, not on whether it has been built.

Method: This Signature design-study proposes a design for Ecosystem Maturity as one working part of the Universal Ecosystem Generator. Its working angle is handover. Its handover is a named handoff: the receiving step gets the specification, the evidence, and the open questions in writing, and the handover is only complete when the receiver can run the checks. All values in this design are illustrative design values, never presented as measured results or as a built system. Scope, as a design: The scope covers ecosystem generation: ecosystem mapping, resource families, connector design, the dependency registry, ecosystem bootstrapping, network growth rules, cross-database links, shared artificial-intelligence services, ecosystem health, adding a new family, retiring a family, ecosystem versioning, ecosystem testing, ecosystem documentation, ecosystem governance, ecosystem metrics, ecosystem templates, ecosystem security, ecosystem accessibility, ecosystem expansion plans, ecosystem failure recovery, ecosystem records, ecosystem maturity, and the ecosystem lifecycle. Outside the scope are the inner workings of the individual resources being connected, which belong to their own databases, and the operation of a finished ecosystem, which belongs to its own records. This database records how ecosystems are generated; it does not duplicate what each resource does alone. Requirements, as design requirements: Every ecosystem-generation method in this database must meet eight requirements. First, it must map the ecosystem before generating it, naming every resource family and every link between families. Second, it must register every dependency explicitly, so a missing resource is visible before generation starts. Third, it must generate complete deliverables for every family, under the paste's section 6 standard, not descriptions of them. Fourth, it must test the generated ecosystem as a connected whole, not only its parts. Fifth, it must define a safe state for a failed generation, so a partial ecosystem is never presented as complete. Sixth, it must version the whole ecosystem together, so the parts and the network agree. Seventh, it must be written in plain words with every technical term defined. Eighth, it must state its maturity and its implementation status plainly, so no reader mistakes a proposal for a finished system. Interfaces, as designed: Ecosystem generation connects through four interfaces. The map interface accepts an ecosystem map: families, resources, and links, each with an identifier. The registry interface records every dependency between resources, so the Master AI can see what depends on what. The generation interface runs the family generators in dependency order and writes each deliverable to the ecosystem record. The health interface checks the finished ecosystem: every link resolves, every resource answers, and every test passes, with the results recorded. All four interfaces use plain text and standard formats, and every generation is stored with its seed and its version, so an ecosystem can be regenerated exactly. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live. The Boundless Generator standard of the paste's section 6 applies to this record: a generator must produce a complete deliverable for its category, not a text description of one. A database generator produces a schema, records, search, an application programming interface, and documentation. A website generator produces pages, navigation, functionality, and a responsive interface. A software generator produces source code, configuration, dependencies, and tests. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. A meta-generator must create and test new generators, not merely describe how one might be created. An ecosystem generator produces connected resources, interfaces, and a dependency registry. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

Results reported: The result of this design-study is a complete proposed design for Ecosystem Maturity, with acceptance tests that say how the design would be judged if it were built. Acceptance for a record in this database is decided by execution and by counting. The demo must run and its output must match the recorded expected output byte for byte, including the line that reads Deterministic: MATCH. The program must run and agree with the demo. The record must carry at least two cited sources, each a real web address verified as reachable at validation time, with a retrieval date. The prose must reach at least eight hundred words, counted over the prose fields and the body. The record must carry a maturity label and an implementation status, the lifecycle steps, the generator standard, and the God-Level and Boundless distinctions in plain words. A record that fails any of these is not accepted, and the failure is recorded, not hidden. Its acceptance tests are the tests in the acceptance paragraph, applied as design criteria. Maturity: E2. Implementation status: proposed, on the paste's section 9 scale of theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, and deployed. Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. The maturity label on this record and its implementation status are two different facts and both are stated: a design can be specified (E3) while its implementation status is still proposed, and a prototype can be tested without being verified or deployed.

When it applies: This design applies whenever someone must propose, review, or teach Ecosystem Maturity inside the Universal Ecosystem Generator: the designer follows the method, the reviewer runs the acceptance tests as design criteria, and the teacher walks the teaching ladder from the simple description to the full design. This database is linked to, never a rebuild of, the live ecosystem-seed database, with 106 records, the live ecosystems database, with 40 records, and the live universal-ecosystem-verification-database pair, with 25 records in each edition, which cover seeds, ecosystems, and verification, not generation. It also links the Foundation program's staged meta-generator, universal-database-generator, and website-generator pairs, package-only, and the Digital Singularity AI pair, package-only, which is the construction engine the paste's section 10 points at.

7. AI

The AI of this database is JAH Universal Ecosystem Librarian (Signature). It explains this database in plain, conversational language, answers questions about any of the 24 records, and can create an AI assistant exactly like itself. It answers from this database's own data only, and says plainly when a question goes beyond it.

  • ai-thing.json — the per-database AI spec (the database's own AI, JAH Universal Ecosystem Librarian (Signature)).
  • ai-discussion.json — the discussion AI spec, built to discuss The Signature Universal Ecosystem Generator Database with a visitor, record by record.

Both are free downloads, free forever, no sign-in.
